People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

I’m here because… I’m having trouble stopping myself from checking my work. Spreadsheets, calculations, that kind of thing. I’ll go over them again and again, even after I know I already checked.

I can stay late for hours because it feels like if I don’t check one more time, something bad could happen and it’d be my fault.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"Even after I know the numbers match, this thought keeps jumping in that I missed one hidden mistake in payroll or taxes and somebody's check won't go through or the firm will get in trouble, and I try to push it away but it comes right back like I can't trust my own eyes.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. If I’m not able to check again, it just sits there in my head, like something bad is going to happen and it’ll be on me.\n\nSo I end up going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain phrases to yourself to try and calm your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really phrases, no. It’s more… I feel like I have to check the spreadsheets or calculations again. If I don’t, I can’t calm down, because it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something in your work feel like they are coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I guess. Not like… a voice or anything outside me.\n\nIt’s more that even when the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed something hidden, like in payroll or taxes, and then I can’t really trust that I saw it right. If I’m not able to check again, it just sits there in my head, like something bad is going to happen and it’ll be on me.\n\nSo I end up going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain phrases to yourself to try and calm your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really phrases, no. It’s more… I feel like I have to check the spreadsheets or calculations again. If I don’t, I can’t calm down, because it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something in your work feel like they are coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I guess. Not like… a voice or anything outside me.\n\nIt’s more that even when the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed something hidden, like in payroll or taxes, and then I can’t really trust that I saw it right. I try to push it away, but it just comes back.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries about missing something or making mistakes mostly limited to your work, or do they also happen in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like with the payroll and tax stuff… even when the numbers match, I keep thinking maybe there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s fine, but it just comes back. Like I can’t trust what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with friends and family due to the time you spend on checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I haven’t really been making it to choir rehearsals or some youth-center things because I can’t leave the office on time.\n\nAnd… I missed a filing deadline once and got called into a formal meeting at work, so it’s not just personal stuff. It’s been affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are also motivated by a need to make sure things are symmetrical or 'just right', beyond just avoiding mistakes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not really like symmetrical or needing it to look a certain way.\n\nIt’s more… I’m worried I missed something. But I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I can’t trust what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with friends and family due to the time you spend on checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I haven’t really been making it to choir rehearsals or some youth-center things because I can’t leave the office on time.\n\nAnd… I missed a filing deadline once and got called into a formal meeting at work, so it’s not just personal stuff. It’s been affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are also motivated by a need to make sure things are symmetrical or 'just right', beyond just avoiding mistakes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not really like symmetrical or needing it to look a certain way.\n\nIt’s more… I’m worried I missed something. But I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking because you're afraid of what might happen if you make a mistake, or because you feel like you need to be in control of everything?\n\nPatient: I think… more because I’m scared of what happens if I’m wrong.\n\nLike, I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even after I already checked them. If I don’t look one more time, it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I stay hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I’m having trouble stopping myself from checking my work. Spreadsheets, calculations, that kind of thing. I’ll go over them again and again, even after I know I already checked.\n\nSometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone leaves. It feels like if I don’t check just one more time, something bad could happen, and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s at work. The spreadsheets and calculations… I just keep going back over them, even when I already checked. I can stay late for hours because it feels like if I don’t check one more time, something bad could happen and it’d be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s usually this thought that I must have missed something, even when the numbers match. Like in payroll or taxes… that one hidden mistake is there and someone’s check won’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble.\n\nI try to just ignore it, but it comes back. It’s like I can’t really trust what I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical habits or actions that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Mostly the checking. I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations… again and again. Even after I already know I checked them.\n\nSometimes I stay for hours after everyone leaves, because it feels like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen… and it’d be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are interfering with your life outside of work or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like saying that, but I missed a filing deadline because I couldn’t get out of the checking, and they called me into a formal meeting about it.\n\nAnd outside work… I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and things I said I’d do at the youth center, because I’m still stuck at the office. It’s… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling anxious or uncomfortable when you are not able to perform your checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I get really uncomfortable. If I’m not able to check again, it just sits there in my head, like something bad is going to happen and it’ll be on me.\n\nSo I end up going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain phrases to yourself to try and calm your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really phrases, no. It’s more… I feel like I have to check the spreadsheets or calculations again. If I don’t, I can’t calm down, because it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something in your work feel like they are coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I guess. Not like… a voice or anything outside me.\n\nIt’s more that even when the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed something hidden, like in payroll or taxes, and then I can’t really trust that I saw it right. I try to push it away, but it just comes back.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries about missing something or making mistakes mostly limited to your work, or do they also happen in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like with the payroll and tax stuff… even when the numbers match, I keep thinking maybe there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s fine, but it just comes back. If I don’t look one more time, it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault. It feels like if I don’t check just one more time, something bad could happen, and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s at work. The spreadsheets and calculations… I just keep going back over them, even when I already checked. I can stay late for hours because it feels like if I don’t check one more time, something bad could happen and it’d be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s usually this thought that I must have missed something, even when the numbers match. Like in payroll or taxes… that one hidden mistake is there and someone’s check won’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble.\n\nI try to just ignore it, but it comes back. It’s like I can’t really trust what I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical habits or actions that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Mostly the checking. I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations… again and again. Even after I already know I checked them.\n\nSometimes I stay for hours after everyone leaves, because it feels like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen… and it’d be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are interfering with your life outside of work or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like saying that, but I missed a filing deadline because I couldn’t get out of the checking, and they called me into a formal meeting about it.\n\nAnd outside work… I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and things I said I’d do at the youth center, because I’m still stuck at the office. It’s… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling anxious or uncomfortable when you are not able to perform your checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I get really uncomfortable. If I’m not able to check again, it just sits there in my head, like something bad is going to happen and it’ll be on me.\n\nSo I end up going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain phrases to yourself to try and calm your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really phrases, no. It’s more… I feel like I have to check the spreadsheets or calculations again. If I don’t, I can’t calm down, because it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something in your work feel like they are coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I guess. Not like… a voice or anything outside me.\n\nIt’s more that even when the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed something hidden, like in payroll or taxes, and then I can’t really trust that I saw it right. I try to push it away, but it just comes back.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries about missing something or making mistakes mostly limited to your work, or do they also happen in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like I can’t trust what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with friends and family due to the time you spend on checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it’s more that I might’ve made a mistake.

I’m not really trying to make it perfect. I just keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations because if I don’t check one more time, it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t stop.

It’s usually this thought that I must have missed something, even when the numbers match. I don’t really like saying that, but I missed a filing deadline because I couldn’t get out of the checking, and they called me into a formal meeting about it.\n\nAnd outside work… I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and things I said I’d do at the youth center, because I’m still stuck at the office. It’s… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling anxious or uncomfortable when you are not able to perform your checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I get really uncomfortable. If I’m not able to check again, it just sits there in my head, like something bad is going to happen and it’ll be on me.\n\nSo I end up going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain phrases to yourself to try and calm your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really phrases, no. It’s more… I feel like I have to check the spreadsheets or calculations again. If I don’t, I can’t calm down, because it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something in your work feel like they are coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I guess. Not like… a voice or anything outside me.\n\nIt’s more that even when the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed something hidden, like in payroll or taxes, and then I can’t really trust that I saw it right. I try to push it away, but it just comes back.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries about missing something or making mistakes mostly limited to your work, or do they also happen in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. I can stay late for hours because it feels like if I don’t check one more time, something bad could happen and it’d be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s usually this thought that I must have missed something, even when the numbers match. Like in payroll or taxes… that one hidden mistake is there and someone’s check won’t go through, or the firm gets in trouble.\n\nI try to just ignore it, but it comes back. It’s like I can’t really trust what I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical habits or actions that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Mostly the checking. I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations… again and again. Even after I already know I checked them.\n\nSometimes I stay for hours after everyone leaves, because it feels like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen… and it’d be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are interfering with your life outside of work or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like saying that, but I missed a filing deadline because I couldn’t get out of the checking, and they called me into a formal meeting about it.\n\nAnd outside work… I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and things I said I’d do at the youth center, because I’m still stuck at the office. It’s… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling anxious or uncomfortable when you are not able to perform your checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I get really uncomfortable. If I’m not able to check again, it just sits there in my head, like something bad is going to happen and it’ll be on me.\n\nSo I end up going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain phrases to yourself to try and calm your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really phrases, no. It’s more… I feel like I have to check the spreadsheets or calculations again. If I don’t, I can’t calm down, because it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something in your work feel like they are coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I guess. Not like… a voice or anything outside me.\n\nIt’s more that even when the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed something hidden, like in payroll or taxes, and then I can’t really trust that I saw it right. I try to push it away, but it just comes back.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries about missing something or making mistakes mostly limited to your work, or do they also happen in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like with the payroll and tax stuff… even when the numbers match, I keep thinking maybe there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s fine, but it just comes back. Like I can’t trust what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with friends and family due to the time you spend on checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I haven’t really been making it to choir rehearsals or some youth-center things because I can’t leave the office on time.\n\nAnd… I missed a filing deadline once and got called into a formal meeting at work, so it’s not just personal stuff. It’s been affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are also motivated by a need to make sure things are symmetrical or 'just right', beyond just avoiding mistakes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not really like symmetrical or needing it to look a certain way.\n\nIt’s more… I’m worried I missed something. But I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking because you're afraid of what might happen if you make a mistake, or because you feel like you need to be in control of everything?\n\nPatient: I think… more because I’m scared of what happens if I’m wrong.\n\nLike, I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even after I already checked them. If I don’t look one more time, it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I stay hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly more about being in control of the situation or avoiding the possibility of making an error?\n\nPatient: It’s more about not making an error, I think.\n\nI don’t really feel like I’m trying to control everything… it’s just, if I don’t check the spreadsheet or the calculation one more time, I get this feeling that something bad will happen and it’ll be on me. So I keep going back over it, even after I already checked. Sometimes I end up staying really late because I can’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is driven more by the fear of something bad happening if you make a mistake, or by the need to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: More the fear of something bad happening if I made a mistake.\n\nIt’s not really about perfect, exactly… I just keep thinking I missed something in the spreadsheet or the calculation. Even if I already checked it, I feel like I have to go back again, or it’ll be my fault if something goes wrong. Sometimes I’m there for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven by the possibility that you might have made a mistake, rather than the desire to ensure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more that I might’ve made a mistake.\n\nI’m not really trying to make it perfect. I just keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations because if I don’t check one more time, it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your checking behaviors are significantly affecting your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I missed a filing deadline, and I got called into a formal meeting about it at work.\n\nAnd I’ve been missing things outside of work too… choir rehearsals, and commitments at the youth center, because I just can’t get out of the office on time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Not really like symmetrical or needing it to look a certain way.\n\nIt’s more… I’m worried I missed something. But I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking because you're afraid of what might happen if you make a mistake, or because you feel like you need to be in control of everything?\n\nPatient: I think… more because I’m scared of what happens if I’m wrong.\n\nLike, I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even after I already checked them. If I don’t look one more time, it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I stay hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly more about being in control of the situation or avoiding the possibility of making an error?\n\nPatient: It’s more about not making an error, I think.\n\nI don’t really feel like I’m trying to control everything… it’s just, if I don’t check the spreadsheet or the calculation one more time, I get this feeling that something bad will happen and it’ll be on me. So I keep going back over it, even after I already checked. Sometimes I end up staying really late because I can’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is driven more by the fear of something bad happening if you make a mistake, or by the need to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: More the fear of something bad happening if I made a mistake.\n\nIt’s not really about perfect, exactly… I just keep thinking I missed something in the spreadsheet or the calculation. Even if I already checked it, I feel like I have to go back again, or it’ll be my fault if something goes wrong. Sometimes I’m there for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven by the possibility that you might have made a mistake, rather than the desire to ensure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more that I might’ve made a mistake.\n\nI’m not really trying to make it perfect. I just keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations because if I don’t check one more time, it feels like something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I can’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your checking behaviors are significantly affecting your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I missed a filing deadline, and I got called into a formal meeting about it at work.\n\nAnd I’ve been missing things outside of work too… choir rehearsals, and commitments at the youth center, because I just can’t get out of the office on time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the distress from not being able to check is overwhelming at times?\n\nPatient: Yes… it can feel really overwhelming.\n\nIf I try to leave without checking again, I just keep thinking something bad is going to happen and it’ll be my fault.
